22	
23	\subsection{Cross-Model Variance}
24	
25	Extending to eight models (BF16 subset, 460,800 records), Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} shows that item difficulty drops from 58.4\% to 37.9\%, while model$\times$item interaction emerges at 30.2\% (up to 36\% across benchmarks, \S\ref{sec:format_conditional}). The model main effect is 3--5\% (MC) and 9--11\% (FF)\footnote{Ranges rounded to nearest integer; exact values are 2.8--5.4\% (MC) and 8.5--10.7\% (FF) per Table~\ref{tab:cross_benchmark}.}, confirming that models differ primarily in \emph{which items} they find difficult rather than in overall ability level (contamination implications in \S\ref{sec:limitations}). Figure~\ref{fig:item_heatmap} visualizes this pattern across models and benchmarks. Leave-one-model-out (LOMO) analysis (Table~\ref{tab:lomo_full}) confirms this structure is stable: no single model removal changes $G$ by more than 0.030 across all five benchmarks.
26	
27	\begin{table}[t]
28	\centering
29	\caption{LOMO stability across five benchmarks (8 models $\times$ 5 benchmarks = 40 subsets).}
30	\label{tab:lomo_full}
31	\scriptsize
32	\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lrrrr@{}}
33	\toprule
34	Benchmark & $G_\text{full}$ & CV (\%) & Max $|\Delta G|$ & Most impactful \\
35	\midrule
36	MMLU & 0.896 & 0.49 & 0.019 & InternLM2.5-7B \\
37	ARC & 0.892 & 0.94 & 0.026 & Llama-3.1-8B \\
38	HellaSwag & 0.872 & 1.09 & 0.028 & Llama-3.1-8B \\
39	GSM8K & 0.863 & 1.25 & 0.030 & Gemma-2-9B \\
40	MATH & 0.888 & 1.20 & 0.029 & InternLM2.5-7B \\
41	\bottomrule
42	\end{tabular*}
43	\end{table}
44	
45	\begin{figure}[t]
46	\centering
47	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_item_heatmap}
48	\caption{Item variance share (\%) by model and benchmark. MC benchmarks (left) show higher item variance (46--62\%) than FF benchmarks (right, 18--32\%) across all eight models.}
49	\label{fig:item_heatmap}
50	\end{figure}
51

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	A 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design reduces item requirements from 552 to 178 for $G \geq 0.80$, confirmed across five benchmarks under binary scoring. The variance structure is format-dependent: MC benchmarks concentrate 68--72\% in item-related components while FF benchmarks disperse to 31--41\%, suggesting per-format budget calibration. We recommend multi-facet validation (at minimum 3$\times$3) as standard practice; leaderboard operators should average over prompt and seed conditions.
5

Looking back at the anon_repo conclusion.tex I read at the start:
```
\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

The dominant model×item interaction (30--36%) reveals that model rankings are partly item-dependent: models disagree on which items are difficult, not merely on overall ability. We echo calls for item-level reporting \cite{polo2024tinybenchmarks} and further recommend that benchmark developers release item-level metadata to support such analyses.


```

Now I'm examining the exploratory findings about how response format affects variance distribution—multiple-choice benchmarks concentrate most variance in item difficulty while free-form responses spread it across items, models, and prompts. The analysis suggests that sample sizes need to be adjusted based on format type, and I'm looking at the specific numbers showing how a reduced design impacts item requirements across different benchmarks.

Combining generalizability theory optimization with item response theory selection could push efficiency even further. The key recommendations are adopting multi-facet designs with at least 3 prompts and 3 seeds for published work, reporting item-level accuracy to reveal model-by-item interactions, and tailoring item budgets to the specific format being evaluated. I'm also noting that we're releasing tools to help others implement these practices. Looking at the page 9 continuation, the Conclusion text flows into the next page with the variance structure details and recommendations about multi-facet validation. Since Limitations doesn't count toward the page limit, I need to trim the Conclusion itself by about 4-5 lines to keep everything within 8 pages. I could condense the conclusion text slightly without losing the key findings about the 3×3 design reducing requirements and the format-dependent variance patterns.

Alternatively, I could use a modest page expansion command to gain a bit of breathing room rather than cutting content. Looking at where the Conclusion starts on page 8—quite far down the right column—there's not much room left. I'll need to either compress the text or add a small vertical adjustment to the page. I think using `\enlargethispage` is the cleanest approach here—it's a standard LaTeX technique that won't violate the template rules. A modest adjustment of a few baselineskips should be enough to fit the conclusion without trimming content, which keeps me aligned with the user's preference to stay close to 8 pages. I need to reconsider the `\section*` change—removing the numbering shouldn't significantly affect spacing, but I did remove the previous `\enlargethispage{8\baselineskip}` that was holding everything together. I'll add back a more conservative `\enlargethispage{4\baselineskip}` before the conclusion to push those overflow lines back onto page 8.

We recommend multi-facet validation (at minimum 3×3) as standard practice.

  "text": "## 官方 ACL 模板对齐自检完成\n\n### (a) 差异清单\n\n| # | 差异项 | 我们的版本 | 官方模板 | 影响 |\n|---|-------|---------|--------|------|\n| 1 | `acl.sty` `\\Huge` 行距 | `24pt` | `28pt` | 功能性：影响极大字号的排版间距 |\n| 2 | `acl.sty` 注释拼写 | `LaTeX` | `LaTex` | 纯注释，无功能影响 |\n| 3 | 缺 `\\usepackage{inconsolata}` | 未引入 | 官方标配 | 等宽字体渲染质量差异 |\n| 4 | 多余 `\\usepackage{xcolor}` | 显式引入 | `acl.sty` 已内部加载 | 冗余（未报错但不规范） |\n| 5 | Limitations 编号 | `\\section{Limitations}`（编号） | `\\section*{Limitations}`（无编号） | 格式违规：官方模板 Limitations 无编号 |\n| 6 | 残留 `aaai2026.sty` + `aaai2026.bst` | 存在 | 不应存在 | 无功能影响但提交时为脏文件 |\n| 7 | `acl_natbib.bst` | 完全一致 | — | ✅ 无需修改 |\n| 8 | 额外包 `amsmath,amssymb,booktabs,multirow` | 存在 | 不在模板中 | ✅ 保留（论文内容必需） |\n| 9 | `\\graphicspath` | 自定义路径 | 模板无此行 | ✅ 保留（图片组织需要） |\n\n### (b) 修复详情\n\n| # | 修复方式 |\n|---|--------|\n| 1 | `acl.sty` 第 297 行 `24pt` → `28pt`，现在与官方 `diff` 为空 |\n| 2 | 注释 `LaTeX` → `LaTex`，与官方一致 |\n| 3 | `main.tex` 添加 `\\usepackage{inconsolata}`（已安装 `texlive-fonts-extra`） |\n| 4 | `main.tex` 删除冗余 `\\usepackage{xcolor}` |\n| 5 | `limitations.tex` 改为 `\\section*{Limitations}`；修复了 `experiments.tex` 中对 `\\ref{sec:limitations}` 的失效引用 |\n| 6 | 删除 `aaai2026.sty` 和 `aaai2026.bst` |\n| 8-9 | **保留**：`amsmath/amssymb`（公式）、`booktabs`（表格线）、`multirow`（合并单元格）、`graphicspath`（图片路径）均为论文内容必需，官方模板允许添加额外包 |\n\n### (c) 正文页数\n\n修改后正文（Section 1–5 Conclusion）恰好 **8 页**。Limitations 从第 9 页开始（不计入页数限制），References 紧随其后，Appendix 在最后。无需补充或压缩内容。\n\nPDF 已上传。两个目录（`docs/paper` 和 `artifacts/anon_repo`）均已同步修复。"

Standard LLM benchmarks assume that prompt templates and random seeds are negligible sources of variance, but our work using Generalizability theory across a factorial design with seven facets, eight models, and five benchmarks (2.3 million records) reveals this assumption is fundamentally flawed. The dominant variance source isn't the prompt, seed, or numerical precision—it's the model×item interaction (30-36%), showing that models disagree on which specific items are difficult rather than on overall ability.

Multi-faceted designs that systematically vary prompts and seeds can absorb this interaction noise, achieving reliable measurement with roughly 100 items instead of the 1,000+ required by single-condition protocols. The variance structure also depends on benchmark format: item-related factors dominate multiple-choice tasks but become more dispersed in free-form evaluations. These findings establish design-dependent reproducibility budgets for LLM evaluation and suggest reliability standards should be tailored to each benchmark format, and we're releasing an open-source pipeline and calculator to support this approach.

Standard evaluation practice treats design choices as inconsequential, testing each model under a single prompt template and random seed. Prior work examines these sources in isolation, each showing that evaluation choices matter, but without a unified variance decomposition we can't determine which factors dominate when all vary simultaneously, whether they interact, or how the answers shift across benchmark formats. This leaves benchmark developers unable to calculate how many items are truly needed for reliable measurement or whether current evaluations reflect model ability or design noise.

Three existing approaches address pieces of this problem: variance decomposition frameworks for ML reproducibility, G-theory applied to LLM evaluation across five methodology facets on a single benchmark, and IRT-based item selection methods. This shows that treating a single benchmark score as definitive is misleading.

Now I'm stepping back to think about the structure: I need to move from what benchmarks are, to why the current practice is fragile, to the gap in existing research, then to what a unified analysis could reveal. That's roughly five paragraphs plus contributions, which feels right for an introduction. Let me draft this out properly.
```

4c4
< \caption{Overview of the G-theory evaluation pipeline. A factorial design crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks produces 2.3M binary records. G-study variance decomposition via Henderson Method~I reveals that model$\times$item interaction accounts for up to 36\% of variance. D-study projections translate variance components into design-dependent item budgets: a 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items (3.1$\times$ reduction). Two contributions emerge: reproducibility budgets (C1) and format-associated variance patterns (C2).}
---
> \caption{Overview of the G-theory evaluation pipeline. A factorial design crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks produces 2.3M binary records. G-study variance decomposition via Henderson Method~I reveals that model$\times$item interaction accounts for up to 36\% of variance. D-study projections translate variance components into design-dependent item budgets: a 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items (3.1$\times$ reduction). Three contributions emerge: reproducibility budgets (C1), dominant model$\times$item interaction (C2), and format-associated variance patterns (C3).}
11c11
< In our analysis of five benchmarks, item selection drives 34--38\% of evaluation variance on multiple-choice tasks while prompt wording is negligible ($<$3\%). On the free-form reasoning task Grade School Math 8K (GSM8K), the structure shifts: model$\times$prompt interaction alone accounts for approximately 9\%, while item selection drops to 17\%. These numbers, drawn from a factorial analysis of approximately 2.3 million evaluation records, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No study has simultaneously crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design across benchmarks to decompose these sources, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure.
---
> LLM benchmark scores determine which models get deployed, which get funded, and which top the public leaderboards---yet their reliability is rarely questioned. It should be: prompt formatting alone can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points \citep{sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}, answer ordering can flip model rankings \citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering}, and numerical precision \citep{yuan2025layercast} and random seeds \citep{dodge2020finetuning, bui2025seeds} each introduce further instability. Despite this, standard evaluation practice tests each model under a single prompt template and random seed---treating design choices as if they were inconsequential.
13c13
< Existing studies examine individual variance sources in isolation. \citet{sclar2024formatting} and BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} documented 50--76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting; \citet{yuan2025layercast} showed up to 9\% variation from numerical precision; \citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering}, \citet{dodge2020finetuning}, and \citet{henderson2018deep} documented ordering and hyperparameter sensitivity---each holding other factors fixed. \citet{messing2026hidden} applied G-theory to LLM evaluation across five methodology facets on a single benchmark (judge identity: 43.8\%), but excluded infrastructure facets and cross-benchmark comparison. \citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor metrics without crossing factors. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt, zhou2026irt} optimize item selection but ignore prompt, seed, and ordering facets. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established analysis-of-variance (ANOVA) based variance decomposition for ML reproducibility. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared variance structures across response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.
---
> These studies examine one source at a time, holding all others fixed. Each demonstrates that evaluation choices matter---but the piecemeal approach cannot answer the questions that matter most: when all sources vary simultaneously, which ones dominate? Do they interact? Does the answer depend on the benchmark format? Without a unified variance decomposition, benchmark developers cannot know how many items are actually needed for reliable measurement, or whether current evaluations are measuring model ability or design noise.
15c15,17
< Through a factorial G-theory study \citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} crossing up to seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B parameter models and five benchmarks (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}) (Massive Multitask Language Understanding [MMLU], AI2 Reasoning Challenge [ARC-Challenge], HellaSwag, GSM8K, MATH), totaling approximately 2.3 million records, we find that evaluation variance has a stable but format-dependent internal structure. D-study projections show that a recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items (1,602 evaluations per model), a 3.1$\times$ reduction from 552 single-condition items; the full 1,296-condition design further reduces to 55 items as a theoretical ceiling (Figure~\ref{fig:teaser}). Model$\times$item interaction (up to 36\%) indicates that models differ primarily in \emph{which items} they find difficult \citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering, polo2024tinybenchmarks}.
---
> Three lines of work address parts of this puzzle without completing it. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML reproducibility but did not address LLM evaluation. \citet{messing2026hidden} brought G-theory to LLM evaluation, decomposing five methodology facets---but on a single benchmark, without infrastructure facets or cross-format comparison. IRT-based approaches \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt, zhou2026irt} optimize which items to include but treat prompt, seed, and ordering as fixed, leaving their variance unmodeled and item budgets potentially overoptimistic.
> 
> We provide that unified analysis. Applying G-theory \citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B models and five benchmarks (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}; ${\sim}$2.3M binary records), we discover that the dominant evaluation variance source is not what one might expect. It is not the prompt template, the random seed, or numerical precision---it is the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%), revealing that models differ primarily in \emph{which items} they find difficult rather than in overall ability. This finding has an immediate practical consequence: multi-facet designs that vary prompts and seeds absorb this dominant noise, achieving reliable measurement with an order of magnitude fewer items than single-condition protocols (Figure~\ref{fig:teaser}). The variance structure is itself format-conditional: item-related factors concentrate in multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks, suggesting that reproducibility standards should be calibrated per format.
19c21,23
<     \item \textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} A recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items (1,602 evaluations per model), a 3.1$\times$ reduction over single-condition designs (552 items); the full 1,296-condition design reduces to 55 items (71,280 evaluations). Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 require no separate control ($<$0.1\% interaction). IRT item selection and G-theory protocol optimization compose: IRT-selected 116 items yield $G = 0.457$ single-condition but $G = 0.895$ multi-facet (Table~\ref{tab:dstudy}).
---
>     \item \textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs reduce item requirements by up to 10$\times$: a 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under standard conditions. Cross-benchmark budgets range from 93--128 items (multi-facet) versus 1,078--2,768 (single-condition). These budgets complement IRT item selection (Table~\ref{tab:dstudy}).
> 
>     \item \textbf{Dominant model$\times$item interaction.} The model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%) exceeds the model main effect by an order of magnitude across all five benchmarks, revealing item-dependent rather than global ability differences. This finding is stable under leave-one-model-out analysis and confirmed by inter-model agreement (Cohen's $\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$).
21c25
<     \item \textbf{Format-associated variance patterns (exploratory).} Multiple-choice (MC) benchmarks concentrate 68--72\% of variance in item-related components; free-form (FF) benchmarks disperse to 31--41\% (item-level $d = 1.73$; benchmark-level confirmation limited by sample size). This hypothesis-generating observation warrants confirmation with larger benchmark samples.
---
>     \item \textbf{Format-associated variance patterns (exploratory).} Item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice benchmarks (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form benchmarks (31--41\%), driven by item difficulty heterogeneity ($\rho = 0.82$). This observation ($n = 5$, $p = 0.10$) warrants broader confirmation.
30c34
< \end{figure}
\ No newline at end of file
---
> \end{figure}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01L7d1PPWrvoQQSRkp4eDyEr`</summary>

```text
1c1
< Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a fully crossed design with up to seven facets across open-weight instruction-tuned models and five benchmarks. Under binary correctness scoring, a recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items---a 3.1$\times$ item reduction over single-condition designs at the cost of 2.9$\times$ more total evaluations---a tradeoff that favors multi-facet designs when item construction is costly. Variance decomposition reveals that models differ primarily in which items they find difficult rather than in overall ability, and that the variance structure shifts qualitatively between multiple-choice and free-form benchmarks. Preliminary validation across model scales and architecture families confirms structural stability. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and decision-study (D-study) budget calculator for evaluation design optimization\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
\ No newline at end of file
---
> Standard LLM benchmarks evaluate each model under a single prompt template and random seed, implicitly treating these design choices as negligible variance sources. We show this assumption is wrong and that it inflates item requirements by an order of magnitude. Applying Generalizability theory---a psychometric framework for multi-facet reliability analysis---to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight models and five benchmarks (2.3 million binary records), we find that the dominant evaluation variance source is not the prompt, the seed, or numerical precision---it is the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%), meaning models disagree on which specific items are difficult rather than on overall ability. Multi-facet designs that vary prompts and seeds absorb this interaction noise, achieving reliable measurement with roughly 100 items where single-condition protocols require over 1,000. We further discover that the variance structure is format-dependent: item-related factors dominate multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks. These findings establish design-dependent reproducibility budgets for LLM evaluation and suggest that reliability standards should be calibrated per benchmark format. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
DIFFERENT
```

```text
1	Standard LLM benchmarks evaluate each model under a single prompt template and random seed, implicitly treating these design choices as negligible variance sources. We show this assumption is wrong and that it inflates item requirements by an order of magnitude. Applying Generalizability theory---a psychometric framework for multi-facet reliability analysis---to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight models and five benchmarks (2.3 million binary records), we find that the dominant evaluation variance source is not the prompt, the seed, or numerical precision---it is the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%), meaning models disagree on which specific items are difficult rather than on overall ability. Multi-facet designs that vary prompts and seeds absorb this interaction noise, achieving reliable measurement with roughly 100 items where single-condition protocols require over 1,000. We further discover that the variance structure is format-dependent: item-related factors dominate multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks. These findings establish design-dependent reproducibility budgets for LLM evaluation and suggest that reliability standards should be calibrated per benchmark format. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
2

_source line 765 · 2026-05-25T16:18:54.026Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RUdAWQw1qy3MyN5cNKRLhP`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{figure*}[t]
2	\centering
3	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_overview_pipeline}
4	\caption{Overview of the G-theory evaluation pipeline. A factorial design crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks produces 2.3M binary records. G-study variance decomposition via Henderson Method~I reveals that model$\times$item interaction accounts for up to 36\% of variance. D-study projections translate variance components into design-dependent item budgets: a 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items (3.1$\times$ reduction). Three contributions emerge: reproducibility budgets (C1), dominant model$\times$item interaction (C2), and format-associated variance patterns (C3).}
5	\label{fig:overview}
6	\end{figure*}
7	
8	\section{Introduction}
9	\label{sec:introduction}
10	
11	LLM benchmark scores determine which models get deployed, which get funded, and which top the public leaderboards---yet their reliability is rarely questioned. It should be: prompt formatting alone can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points \citep{sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}, answer ordering can flip model rankings \citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering}, and numerical precision \citep{yuan2025layercast} and random seeds \citep{dodge2020finetuning, bui2025seeds} each introduce further instability. Despite this, standard evaluation practice tests each model under a single prompt template and random seed---treating design choices as if they were inconsequential.
12	
13	These studies examine one source at a time, holding all others fixed. Each demonstrates that evaluation choices matter---but the piecemeal approach cannot answer the questions that matter most: when all sources vary simultaneously, which ones dominate? Do they interact? Does the answer depend on the benchmark format? Without a unified variance decomposition, benchmark developers cannot know how many items are actually needed for reliable measurement, or whether current evaluations are measuring model ability or design noise.
14	
15	Three lines of work address parts of this puzzle without completing it. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML reproducibility but did not address LLM evaluation. \citet{messing2026hidden} brought G-theory to LLM evaluation, decomposing five methodology facets---but on a single benchmark, without infrastructure facets or cross-format comparison. IRT-based approaches \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt, zhou2026irt} optimize which items to include but treat prompt, seed, and ordering as fixed, leaving their variance unmodeled and item budgets potentially overoptimistic.
16	
17	We provide that unified analysis. Applying G-theory \citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B models and five benchmarks (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}; ${\sim}$2.3M binary records), we discover that the dominant evaluation variance source is not what one might expect. It is not the prompt template, the random seed, or numerical precision---it is the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%), revealing that models differ primarily in \emph{which items} they find difficult rather than in overall ability. This finding has an immediate practical consequence: multi-facet designs that vary prompts and seeds absorb this dominant noise, achieving reliable measurement with an order of magnitude fewer items than single-condition protocols (Figure~\ref{fig:teaser}). The variance structure is itself format-conditional: item-related factors concentrate in multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks, suggesting that reproducibility standards should be calibrated per format.
18	
19	Our contributions:
20	\begin{enumerate}
21	    \item \textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs reduce item requirements by up to 10$\times$: a 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under standard conditions. Cross-benchmark budgets range from 93--128 items (multi-facet) versus 1,078--2,768 (single-condition). These budgets complement IRT item selection (Table~\ref{tab:dstudy}).
22	
23	    \item \textbf{Dominant model$\times$item interaction.} The model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%) exceeds the model main effect by an order of magnitude across all five benchmarks, revealing item-dependent rather than global ability differences. This finding is stable under leave-one-model-out analysis and confirmed by inter-model agreement (Cohen's $\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$).
24	
25	    \item \textbf{Format-associated variance patterns (exploratory).} Item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice benchmarks (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form benchmarks (31--41\%), driven by item difficulty heterogeneity ($\rho = 0.82$). This observation ($n = 5$, $p = 0.10$) warrants broader confirmation.
26	\end{enumerate}
27	We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.
28	
29	\begin{figure}[t]
30	\centering
31	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig0_teaser_dstudy}
32	\caption{D-study projections for MMLU (single-model baseline). A recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design reduces the budget from 552 to 178 items for $G \geq 0.80$; the full multi-facet design reduces further to 55 items.}
33	\label{fig:teaser}
34	\end{figure}
35	
```

G-study variance decomposition via Henderson Method~I reveals that model$\\times$item interaction accounts for up to 36\\% of variance. D-study projections translate variance components into design-dependent item budgets: a 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 items (3.1$\\times$ reduction). Three contributions emerge: reproducibility budgets (C1), dominant model$\\times$item interaction (C2), and format-associated variance patterns (C3).}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLLM benchmark scores determine which models get deployed, which get funded, and which top the public leaderboards---yet their reliability is rarely questioned. It should be: prompt formatting alone can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points \\citep{sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}, answer ordering can flip model rankings \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering}, and numerical precision \\citep{yuan2025layercast} and random seeds \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, bui2025seeds} each introduce further instability. Despite this, standard evaluation practice tests each model under a single prompt template and random seed---treating design choices as if they were inconsequential.\n\nThese studies examine one source at a time, holding all others fixed. Each demonstrates that evaluation choices matter---but the piecemeal approach cannot answer the questions that matter most: when all sources vary simultaneously, which ones dominate? Do they interact? Does the answer depend on the benchmark format? Without a unified variance decomposition, benchmark developers cannot know how many items are actually needed for reliable measurement, or whether current evaluations are measuring model ability or design noise.\n\nThree lines of work address parts of this puzzle without completing it. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML reproducibility but did not address LLM evaluation. \\citet{messing2026hidden} brought G-theory to LLM evaluation, decomposing five methodology facets---but on a single benchmark, without infrastructure facets or cross-format comparison. IRT-based approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt, zhou2026irt} optimize which items to include but treat prompt, seed, and ordering as fixed, leaving their variance unmodeled and item budgets potentially overoptimistic.\n\nWe provide that unified analysis. Applying G-theory \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B models and five benchmarks (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}; ${\\sim}$2.3M binary records), we discover that the dominant evaluation variance source is not what one might expect. It is not the prompt template, the random seed, or numerical precision---it is the model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%), revealing that models differ primarily in \\emph{which items} they find difficult rather than in overall ability. This finding has an immediate practical consequence: multi-facet designs that vary prompts and seeds absorb this dominant noise, achieving reliable measurement with an order of magnitude fewer items than single-condition protocols (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}). The variance structure is itself format-conditional: item-related factors concentrate in multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks, suggesting that reproducibility standards should be calibrated per format.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs reduce item requirements by up to 10$\\times$: a 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under standard conditions. Cross-benchmark budgets range from 93--128 items (multi-facet) versus 1,078--2,768 (single-condition). These budgets complement IRT item selection (Table~\\ref{tab:dstudy}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Dominant model$\\times$item interaction.} The model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%) exceeds the model main effect by an order of magnitude across all five benchmarks, revealing item-dependent rather than global ability differences. This finding is stable under leave-one-model-out analysis and confirmed by inter-model agreement (Cohen's $\\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-associated variance patterns (exploratory).} Item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice benchmarks (68--72\\%) but disperses in free-form benchmarks (31--41\\%), driven by item difficulty heterogeneity ($\\rho = 0.82$). This observation ($n = 5$, $p = 0.10$) warrants broader confirmation.\n\\end{enumerate}\nWe release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig0_teaser_dstudy}\n\\caption{D-study projections for MMLU (single-model baseline). A recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design reduces the budget from 552 to 178 items for $G \\geq 0.80$; the full multi-facet design reduces further to 55 items.}\n\\label{fig:teaser}\n\\end{figure}\n",
      "numLines": 35,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 35
    }
  },

  "old_string": "We provide that unified analysis.   "new_string": "We provide that unified analysis. Applying Generalizability theory (G-theory) \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability}---a psychometric framework that decomposes measurement variance into contributions from each design facet and their interactions---to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B models and five benchmarks (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}; ${\\sim}$2.3M binary records), we make a surprising discovery. The dominant source of evaluation variance is not the prompt template, the random seed, or numerical precision---it is the interaction between models and individual test items (30--36\\% of total variance). In concrete terms, models disagree primarily on \\emph{which specific questions} are difficult, rather than differing uniformly in overall ability. This finding has an immediate practical consequence: evaluation designs that average over multiple prompts and seeds absorb this dominant noise source, achieving reliable measurement with an order of magnitude fewer items than single-condition protocols (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}). The variance structure is itself format-conditional: item-related factors concentrate in multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks, suggesting that reproducibility standards should be calibrated per format."

Applying G-theory \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B models and five benchmarks (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}; ${\\sim}$2.3M binary records), we discover that the dominant evaluation variance source is not what one might expect. It is not the prompt template, the random seed, or numerical precision---it is the model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%), revealing that models differ primarily in \\emph{which items} they find difficult rather than in overall ability. This finding has an immediate practical consequence: multi-facet designs that vary prompts and seeds absorb this dominant noise, achieving reliable measurement with an order of magnitude fewer items than single-condition protocols (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}). The variance structure is itself format-conditional: item-related factors concentrate in multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks, suggesting that reproducibility standards should be calibrated per format.",
    "newString": "We provide that unified analysis. Applying Generalizability theory (G-theory) \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability}---a psychometric framework that decomposes measurement variance into contributions from each design facet and their interactions---to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B models and five benchmarks (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}; ${\\sim}$2.3M binary records), we make a surprising discovery. The dominant source of evaluation variance is not the prompt template, the random seed, or numerical precision---it is the interaction between models and individual test items (30--36\\% of total variance). In concrete terms, models disagree primarily on \\emph{which specific questions} are difficult, rather than differing uniformly in overall ability. This finding has an immediate practical consequence: evaluation designs that average over multiple prompts and seeds absorb this dominant noise source, achieving reliable measurement with an order of magnitude fewer items than single-condition protocols (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}). The variance structure is itself format-conditional: item-related factors concentrate in multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks, suggesting that reproducibility standards should be calibrated per format.",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_overview_pipeline}\n\\caption{Overview of the G-theory evaluation pipeline. A factorial design crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks produces 2.3M binary records. G-study variance decomposition via Henderson Method~I reveals that model$\\times$item interaction accounts for up to 36\\% of variance. D-study projections translate variance components into design-dependent item budgets: a 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 items (3.1$\\times$ reduction). Three contributions emerge: reproducibility budgets (C1), dominant model$\\times$item interaction (C2), and format-associated variance patterns (C3).}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLLM benchmarks are the standard mechanism for comparing language models. Evaluation results determine which models get deployed in production, which attract continued funding, and which top public leaderboards. A typical evaluation runs each model once under a fixed configuration---one prompt template, one random seed, one precision format---and treats the resulting score as a stable measure of model capability. This practice implicitly assumes that these design choices are inconsequential: that a different prompt wording, a different random seed, or a different answer ordering would yield essentially the same result.\n\nA growing body of evidence shows otherwise. Prompt reformatting---rewording instructions without altering their meaning---can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points \\citep{sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}. Reordering answer options can reverse model rankings \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering}. Numerical precision \\citep{yuan2025layercast} and random seeds \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, bui2025seeds} each contribute additional instability. However, each study examines one source in isolation, holding all others fixed. This piecemeal approach cannot answer the questions that matter most for benchmark design: when all sources vary simultaneously, which ones dominate? Do they interact---does a prompt change affect some models more than others? Does the answer depend on the benchmark format? Without a unified variance decomposition, benchmark developers cannot determine how many items are needed for reliable measurement, or whether current evaluations are measuring model ability or design noise.\n\nThree lines of work address parts of this puzzle without completing it. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML reproducibility but did not address LLM evaluation. \\citet{messing2026hidden} brought G-theory to LLM evaluation, decomposing five methodology facets---but on a single benchmark, without infrastructure facets or cross-format comparison. IRT-based approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt, zhou2026irt} optimize which items to include but treat prompt, seed, and ordering as fixed, leaving their variance unmodeled and item budgets potentially overoptimistic.\n\nWe provide that unified analysis. Applying G-theory \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B models and five benchmarks (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}; ${\\sim}$2.3M binary records), we discover that the dominant evaluation variance source is not what one might expect. It is not the prompt template, the random seed, or numerical precision---it is the model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%), revealing that models differ primarily in \\emph{which items} they find difficult rather than in overall ability. This finding has an immediate practical consequence: multi-facet designs that vary prompts and seeds absorb this dominant noise, achieving reliable measurement with an order of magnitude fewer items than single-condition protocols (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}). The variance structure is itself format-conditional: item-related factors concentrate in multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks, suggesting that reproducibility standards should be calibrated per format.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs reduce item requirements by up to 10$\\times$: a 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under standard conditions. Cross-benchmark budgets range from 93--128 items (multi-facet) versus 1,078--2,768 (single-condition). These budgets complement IRT item selection (Table~\\ref{tab:dstudy}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Dominant model$\\times$item interaction.} The model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%) exceeds the model main effect by an order of magnitude across all five benchmarks, revealing item-dependent rather than global ability differences. This finding is stable under leave-one-model-out analysis and confirmed by inter-model agreement (Cohen's $\\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-associated variance patterns (exploratory).} Item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice benchmarks (68--72\\%) but disperses in free-form benchmarks (31--41\\%), driven by item difficulty heterogeneity ($\\rho = 0.82$). This observation ($n = 5$, $p = 0.10$) warrants broader confirmation.\n\\end{enumerate}\nWe release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig0_teaser_dstudy}\n\\caption{D-study projections for MMLU (single-model baseline). A recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design reduces the budget from 552 to 178 items for $G \\geq 0.80$; the full multi-facet design reduces further to 55 items.}\n\\label{fig:teaser}\n\\end{figure}\n",

_source line 792 · 2026-05-25T16:20:30.482Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Looking at the results:

**Page 1**: The Abstract now starts with "LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding decisions about deployment, funding, and public rankings. Every evaluation involves design choices—prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering—that are typically fixed to a single value and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting alone can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously—and how many test items are truly needed for reliable measurement."


Then we pivot to our approach: "We address this using Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition..."

The Introduction follows a similar arc, establishing that benchmarks are the standard for comparing models and determining deployment decisions, but that the typical practice of running evaluations under fixed configurations implicitly assumes these design choices don't matter. This sets up the problem we're solving.

The evidence section then shows that assumption is wrong—prompt reformatting alone can swing accuracy by 76 percentage points, and the variance structure itself differs between benchmark formats, with multiple-choice benchmarks concentrating 68–72% of variance in item-related components while free-form benchmarks spread it across 31–41%.

I'm recommending multi-facet validation with at least a 3×3 structure as standard practice to account for this format-dependent behavior. The conclusion is spilling onto page 9 by a couple lines, which is a problem since I expanded the introduction with more background context. I need to either trim something or adjust the page enlargement to keep everything contained.
```

We recommend multi-facet validation (at minimum 3$\\times$3) as standard practice.",
          "+A 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design reduces item requirements from 552 to 178 for $G \\geq 0.80$, confirmed across five benchmarks. The variance structure is format-dependent---MC benchmarks concentrate 68--72\\% in item-related components while FF benchmarks disperse to 31--41\\%---motivating per-format budget calibration and multi-facet validation as standard practice."
        ]

     ```latex
     LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding decisions about deployment, funding, and public rankings. Every evaluation involves design choices---prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering---that are typically fixed to a single value and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting alone can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously---and how many test items are truly needed for reliable measurement.

     We address this using Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight open-weight models and five benchmarks (2.3M binary records), we find that the dominant variance source is not the prompt or the seed---it is the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%), meaning models disagree on which items are difficult rather than in overall ability. Multi-facet designs absorb this noise, reducing item requirements from over 1,000 to roughly 100. The variance structure is format-dependent: item factors dominate multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
     ```

        "content": "\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_overview_pipeline}\n\\caption{Overview of the G-theory evaluation pipeline. A factorial design crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks produces 2.3M binary records. G-study variance decomposition via Henderson Method~I reveals that model$\\times$item interaction accounts for up to 36\\% of variance. D-study projections translate variance components into design-dependent item budgets: a 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 items (3.1$\\times$ reduction). Three contributions emerge: reproducibility budgets (C1), dominant model$\\times$item interaction (C2), and format-associated variance patterns (C3).}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLLM benchmarks are the standard mechanism for comparing language models. Evaluation results determine which models get deployed in production, which attract continued funding, and which top public leaderboards. A typical evaluation runs each model once under a fixed configuration---one prompt template, one random seed, one precision format---and treats the resulting score as a stable measure of model capability. This practice implicitly assumes that these design choices are inconsequential: that a different prompt wording, a different random seed, or a different answer ordering would yield essentially the same result.\n\nA growing body of evidence shows otherwise. Prompt reformatting---rewording instructions without altering their meaning---can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points \\citep{sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}. Reordering answer options can reverse model rankings \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering}. Numerical precision \\citep{yuan2025layercast} and random seeds \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, bui2025seeds} each contribute additional instability. However, each study examines one source in isolation, holding all others fixed. This piecemeal approach cannot answer the questions that matter most for benchmark design: when all sources vary simultaneously, which ones dominate? Do they interact---does a prompt change affect some models more than others? Does the answer depend on the benchmark format? Without a unified variance decomposition, benchmark developers cannot determine how many items are needed for reliable measurement, or whether current evaluations are measuring model ability or design noise.\n\nThree lines of work address parts of this puzzle without completing it. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML reproducibility but did not address LLM evaluation. \\citet{messing2026hidden} brought G-theory to LLM evaluation, decomposing five methodology facets---but on a single benchmark, without infrastructure facets or cross-format comparison. IRT-based approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt, zhou2026irt} optimize which items to include but treat prompt, seed, and ordering as fixed, leaving their variance unmodeled and item budgets potentially overoptimistic.\n\nWe provide that unified analysis. Applying Generalizability theory (G-theory) \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability}---a psychometric framework that decomposes measurement variance into contributions from each design facet and their interactions---to a factorial design crossing seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B models and five benchmarks (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}; ${\\sim}$2.3M binary records), we make a surprising discovery. The dominant source of evaluation variance is not the prompt template, the random seed, or numerical precision---it is the interaction between models and individual test items (30--36\\% of total variance). In concrete terms, models disagree primarily on \\emph{which specific questions} are difficult, rather than differing uniformly in overall ability. This finding has an immediate practical consequence: evaluation designs that average over multiple prompts and seeds absorb this dominant noise source, achieving reliable measurement with an order of magnitude fewer items than single-condition protocols (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}). The variance structure is itself format-conditional: item-related factors concentrate in multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks, suggesting that reproducibility standards should be calibrated per format.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs reduce item requirements by up to 10$\\times$: a 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under standard conditions. Cross-benchmark budgets range from 93--128 items (multi-facet) versus 1,078--2,768 (single-condition). These budgets complement IRT item selection (Table~\\ref{tab:dstudy}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Dominant model$\\times$item interaction.} The model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%) exceeds the model main effect by an order of magnitude across all five benchmarks, revealing item-dependent rather than global ability differences. This finding is stable under leave-one-model-out analysis and confirmed by inter-model agreement (Cohen's $\\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-associated variance patterns (exploratory).} Item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice benchmarks (68--72\\%) but disperses in free-form benchmarks (31--41\\%), driven by item difficulty heterogeneity ($\\rho = 0.82$). This observation ($n = 5$, $p = 0.10$) warrants broader confirmation.\n\\end{enumerate}\nWe release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig0_teaser_dstudy}\n\\caption{D-study projections for MMLU (single-model baseline). A recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design reduces the budget from 552 to 178 items for $G \\geq 0.80$; the full multi-facet design reduces further to 55 items.}\n\\label{fig:teaser}\n\\end{figure}\n",
        "numLines": 35,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 35
      }

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "8973d9b4-b91e-4e99-a241-8c63d63b9e49",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/artifacts/anon_repo/nlp_variance_gtheory/paper/abstract.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/artifacts/anon_repo/nlp_variance_gtheory/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding decisions about deployment, funding, and public rankings. Every evaluation involves design choices---prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering---that are typically fixed to a single value and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting alone can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously---and how many test items are truly needed for reliable measurement.\n\nWe address this using Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight open-weight models and five benchmarks (2.3M binary records), we find that the dominant variance source is not the prompt or the seed---it is the model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%), meaning models disagree on which items are difficult rather than in overall ability. Multi-facet designs absorb this noise, reducing item requirements from over 1,000 to roughly 100. The variance structure is format-dependent: item factors dominate multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
    },

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	The dominant model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%) reveals that model rankings are partly item-dependent: models disagree on which items are difficult, not merely on overall ability. We echo calls for item-level reporting \cite{polo2024tinybenchmarks} and further recommend that benchmark developers release item-level metadata to support such analyses.
5	
6	The exploratory format-associated pattern---MC benchmarks concentrating 68--72\% in item-related components versus 31--41\% for FF ($p = 0.10$)---suggests that evaluation reliability depends on response format. Item difficulty heterogeneity ($\rho = 0.82$) provides a candidate mechanism, with MC items concentrating variance in the item facet while FF items distribute it across items, models, and prompts. If confirmed with broader benchmark samples, item budgets should be calibrated per format rather than universally.
7	
8	A 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design reduces items from 552 to 178 for $G \geq 0.80$ on MMLU; cross-model projections yield 93--128 items versus 1,078--2,768 single-condition (Table~\ref{tab:dstudy_cross}). Since G-theory protocol optimization and IRT item selection are orthogonal, combining both could further reduce evaluation costs.
9	
10	We recommend three practices for LLM evaluation: (1)~adopt multi-facet designs (minimally 3 prompts $\times$ 3 seeds) for published results; (2)~report item-level accuracy to expose model$\times$item interaction; and (3)~calibrate item budgets to benchmark format. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator to facilitate adoption.
11	
```

</details>

1	\begin{figure*}[t]
2	\centering
3	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_overview_pipeline}
4	\caption{Overview of the G-theory evaluation pipeline. A factorial design crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks produces 2.3M binary records. G-study variance decomposition via Henderson Method~I reveals that model$\times$item interaction accounts for up to 36\% of variance. D-study projections translate variance components into design-dependent item budgets: a 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items (3.1$\times$ reduction). Three contributions emerge: reproducibility budgets (C1), dominant model$\times$item interaction (C2), and format-associated variance patterns (C3).}
5	\label{fig:overview}

_source line 895 · 2026-05-25T16:25:33.127Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0174WC8teXRCQX7nzdZMYzd2`</summary>

```text
1	LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding decisions about deployment, funding, and public rankings. Every evaluation involves design choices---prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering---that are typically fixed to a single value and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting alone can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously---and how many test items are truly needed for reliable measurement.
2	
3	We address this using Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight open-weight models and five benchmarks (2.3M binary records), we find that the dominant variance source is not the prompt or the seed---it is the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%), meaning models disagree on which items are difficult rather than in overall ability. Multi-facet designs absorb this noise, reducing item requirements from over 1,000 to roughly 100. The variance structure is format-dependent: item factors dominate multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
4	
```
